=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two strings word1 and word2.
A string x is called almost equal to y if you can change at most one character in x to make it identical to y.
A sequence of indices seq is called valid if:

The indices are sorted in ascending order.
Concatenating the characters at these indices in word1 in the same order results in a string that is almost equal to word2.

Return an array of size word2.length representing the lexicographically smallest valid sequence of indices. If no such sequence of indices exists, return an empty array.
Note that the answer must represent the lexicographically smallest array, not the corresponding string formed by those indices.
 
Example 1:

Input: word1 = "vbcca", word2 = "abc"
Output: [0,1,2]
Explanation:
The lexicographically smallest valid sequence of indices is [0, 1, 2]:

Change word1[0] to 'a'.
word1[1] is already 'b'.
word1[2] is already 'c'.


Example 2:

Input: word1 = "bacdc", word2 = "abc"
Output: [1,2,4]
Explanation:
The lexicographically smallest valid sequence of indices is [1, 2, 4]:

word1[1] is already 'a'.
Change word1[2] to 'b'.
word1[4] is already 'c'.


Example 3:

Input: word1 = "aaaaaa", word2 = "aaabc"
Output: []
Explanation:
There is no valid sequence of indices.

Example 4:

Input: word1 = "abc", word2 = "ab"
Output: [0,1]

 
Constraints:

1 <= word2.length < word1.length <= 3 * 10^5
word1 and word2 consist only of lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def validSequence(self, word1: str, word2: str) -> List[int]:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

(and i > prev). Need choose smallest i > prev satisfying. But is that sufficient? What if word1[i]==word2[k] and L1[k+1] maybe < latest_start[k+1]? Actually L1[k+1] >= latest_start[k+1]? Since with <=1 mismatch includes exact, so L1[k+1] >= latest_start[k+1]? Need check. L1 is max start among <=1 mismatch, exact occurrences included, so L1 >= latest_start. Yes. So exact match threshold is larger/equal. Mismatch threshold latest_start. If char equal, using exact match leaves one mismatch for suffix; condition i < L1[k+1]. If char !=, mismatch used, exact suffix condition i < latest_start[k+1]. Good.

But is there also possibility if char equal, we could treat it as mismatch? No, if char equal and we "use mismatch" at current position, then remaining exact suffix; but that would increase mismatch count by 1 despite char equal? The definition of almost equal: change at most one character in selected string. If selected char equals target, changing it would be unnecessary; Hamming distance counts mismatches only. We cannot count equal as mismatch. So if char equal and rem=1, choosing it doesn't consume mismatch; remaining rem=1. If char !=, choosing it consumes mismatch; remaining rem=0. If rem=0 and char !=, invalid. Good.

Now the greedy algorithm:
Initialize prev = -1, k=0, rem=1.
While k < m:
  Find smallest i from prev+1 to n-1 such that condition holds.
  If none return [].
  Append i.
  If word1[i] != word2[k]: rem = 0 (since rem must be 1; if rem=0 invalid)
  prev = i; k += 1.

Need ensure lexicographically smallest sequence. Greedy choosing smallest feasible i at each step should yield lexicographically smallest index vector because lex order: at first differing index, smaller is better. If choose smallest feasible first, any valid sequence with smaller first impossible by definition; then recursively. Need ensure feasibility oracle exact. Yes.

Now complexity: Need find smallest i > prev satisfying condition efficiently. Naively scanning i from prev+1 until condition true could be O(n*m) if we scan many positions for each k? But total scanned positions across k? Since prev increases, if we scan from prev+1 and stop at chosen i, then total scanned indices across all k is at most n? Wait for each k, we might scan from prev+1 to chosen i; chosen i is appended and becomes prev. Next k starts at prev+1. So each index is considered at most once? Let's examine: At step k, we scan i = prev+1, prev+2, ... until find valid. The chosen i is appended; next step starts at i+1. So indeed each index in word1 is examined at most once across all steps if we do linear scan and stop at first valid. But what if no valid at current k and we scan to n? Then return empty. So total O(n). But wait we need to test condition for each i; condition uses precomputed arrays and char compare O(1). So total O(n). Is it that simple? Need be careful: Greedy selecting first feasible i at each k with rem may not require scanning beyond chosen; yes. But is it always optimal to choose the first feasible i even if it consumes mismatch? Lexicographically smallest index at current position, yes, if feasible. If first feasible uses mismatch and later feasible exact with same index? At same index char fixed; if char != target, it consumes mismatch. If there is a later index that is larger but leaves more rem? Lexicographic prioritizes smaller current index, so choose smaller even if it consumes mismatch, as long as completion exists. Good.

Now, is L1 recurrence correct? Let's formalize.

Define exact_start[k] = latest_start[k] = maximum starting index p such that word2[k:] is a subsequence of word1[p:]. For k=m, exact_start[m]=n (empty suffix can start at n? For L1[k]:

  T_exact = L1[k+1]  # for exact first

Now, need prove L1[k] as max start for <=1 mismatch. Let's more rigorously examine recurrence. For suffix word2[k:] (length l), any valid occurrence with <=1 mismatch has first selected index i. Cases:
- If word1[i] == word2[k], then remaining suffix word2[k+1:] must be matched with <=1 mismatch in word1[i+1:]. There exists such iff L1[k+1] >= i+1, i.e., i < L1[k+1]. Conversely if i < L1[k+1], the occurrence of remaining suffix starting at L1[k+1] is after i, and first char exact. So valid. Thus any i with char equal and i < L1[k+1] is a valid first index. To maximize i among exact-first starts, choose largest i < L1[k+1] with char equal. cand1.
- If word1[i] != word2[k], then mismatch used, remaining suffix must be matched exactly in word1[i+1:]. Exists iff exact_start[k+1] >= i+1, i.e., i < exact_start[k+1]. Thus valid mismatch-first starts are i with char != and i < exact_start[k+1]. Max is cand2.
Then max first index L1[k] = max(cand1,cand2). If no valid, -1. Base empty suffix: L1[m]=n because empty suffix can be considered to start at n? For recurrence, i < n always. Also exact_start[m]=n. Good.

Need ensure if L1[k+1] = -1, cand1 -1. If exact_start[k+1] = -1, cand2 -1. Good.

Now, greedy feasibility condition details:
At step k with rem=1, prev index p. We scan i from p+1 upward. Need choose smallest i such that:
- if word1[i] == word2[k]: i < L1[k+1] (since rem stays 1)
- else: i < exact_start[k+1] (since rem becomes 0)
Is this exactly F(i+1,k+1,rem_after)? Let's verify with definitions:
If word1[i]==word2[k], after choosing i, remaining rem=1. Need word2[k+1:] match in word1[i+1:] with <=1 mismatch. F(pos=i+1,k+1,1) iff L1[k+1] >= i+1 iff i < L1[k+1]. Yes.
If word1[i]!=word2[k], after choosing i, remaining rem=0. Need exact match word2[k+1:] in word1[i+1:]. F(pos=i+1,k+1,0) iff exact_start[k+1] >= i+1 iff i < exact_start[k+1]. Yes.
For rem=0:
- if word1[i] != word2[k]: invalid.
- if equal: need exact suffix after i: i < exact_start[k+1].
Thus condition.

But wait, if rem=1 and word1[i]==word2[k], could we also choose to "use" mismatch later; yes condition L1[k+1] accounts. If L1[k+1] maybe achieved by an occurrence that itself uses a mismatch at some position, okay. If word1[i]!=word2[k], we must use mismatch now; remaining exact. Good.

Now, one subtlety: The definition "change at most one character in x to make it identical to y" means if x already equals y, zero changes. Hamming distance <=1. Our rem=1 includes zero mismatch. Good.

Another subtlety: The sequence indices sorted ascending and concatenating chars at these indices in word1 in same order. We are selecting a subsequence. Our DP treats selected positions sorted. Good.

Now, need compute exact_start and L1 arrays. We can compute with positions_by_char and prev_diff. Let's design code.

Input word1, word2. n=len(word1), m=len(word2). m < n per constraints but maybe if m>=n? Problem says m<n. But code can handle? If m > n impossible, but constraints no. We can still.

Precompute positions_by_char: list of 26 lists of indices.
prev_diff: list length n. We can compute while scanning word1:
last_pos = [-1]*26
prev_diff = [-1]*n
for i,ch in enumerate(word1):
    c = ord(ch)-97
    best = -1
    for d in range(26):
        if d != c and last_pos[d] > best:
            best = last_pos[d]
    prev_diff[i] = best
    last_pos[c] = i
This O(26n). Could optimize with top two last positions:
Maintain largest last_pos and its char, second largest with different char. For each i c:
  prev_diff[i] = largest last_pos among chars != c. If largest char == c, use second largest; else largest.
Then update. But O(26n) simpler and 7.8M okay. But Python in LeetCode maybe 3e5*26 = 7.8 million, fine. Actually 7.8 million loops, okay. Need also bisect. We'll maybe implement top two for efficiency and elegance.

Let's implement top two:
last = [-1]*26
max1_pos = -1; max1_char = -1; max2_pos = -1
For each i, ch:
  c = ord(ch)-97
  if max1_char == c:
      prev_diff[i] = max2_pos  # largest position with char != c
  else:
      prev_diff[i] = max1_pos
  # update last[c]=i
  last[c]=i
  if i > max1_pos:
      if c != max1_char:
          max2_pos = max1_pos
          max1_pos = i
          max1_char = c
      else:
          max1_pos = i
  elif i > max2_pos and c != max1_char:
      max2_pos = i
Need be careful if c == max1_char and i > max1_pos, max1_pos updated, max2 unchanged. If c != max1_char and i > max1_pos, old max1 becomes max2. If c != max1_char and i <= max1_pos but i > max2_pos, update max2. If c == max1_char and i <= max1_pos, no update. This maintains top two positions with different chars. Let's test. Initially max1_pos=-1, max1_char=-1, max2_pos=-1. For first char c:
prev_diff[i] = if max1_char==c? -1==c? false if c>=0, so max1_pos=-1. update last. if i > max1_pos (-1): c != max1_char (-1) true, max2_pos=max1_pos(-1), max1_pos=0, max1_char=c. Good.
Second char same c at i=1:
prev_diff: max1_char==c, prev_diff=max2_pos=-1 (no different before). update last[c]=1. if i > max1_pos (1>0) and c==max1_char, max1_pos=1. max2 remains -1. Good.
Second char different d at i=1:
prev_diff: max1_char!=d, prev_diff=max1_pos=0 (last different before is previous c). update. if i>max1_pos (1>0) and c!=max1_char, max2_pos=max1_pos(0), max1_pos=1, max1_char=d. Good.
Third char same as max1 d at i=2:
prev_diff=max2_pos=0 (largest different before is c at0). update max1_pos=2. Good.
Third char c at i=2 (different from max1 d):
prev_diff=max1_pos=1 (d at1). update last[c]=2. if i>max1_pos (2>1) and c!=max1_char, max2_pos=max1_pos=1, max1_pos=2,max1_char=c. Good. Top two positions with different chars maintained. Now positions_by_char:
pos = [[] for _ in range(26)]
for i,ch in enumerate(word1): pos[ord(ch)-97].append(i)

Compute exact_start array length m+1:
exact = [0]*(m+1)
exact[m] = n
from bisect import bisect_left
for k in range(m-1, -1, -1):
    c = ord(word2[k])-97
    T = exact[k+1]
    lst = pos[c]
    idx = bisect_left(lst, T) - 1
    exact[k] = lst[idx] if idx >= 0 else -1
Because need largest index < T. bisect_left returns first >= T, so idx before. If T = -1, bisect_left(lst,-1)=0, idx=-1. Good.

Compute L1 array length m+1:
L1 = [0]*(m+1)
L1[m] = n
for k in range(m-1, -1, -1):
    c = ord(word2[k])-97
    T_exact = L1[k+1]
    T_mismatch = exact[k+1]
    # cand1
    idx = bisect_left(pos[c], T_exact) - 1
    cand1 = pos[c][idx] if idx >= 0 else -1
    # cand2
    cand2 = -1
    if T_mismatch > 0:
        p = T_mismatch - 1
        if word1[p] != word2[k]: # char code? compare chars or ord
            cand2 = p
        else:
            cand2 = prev_diff[p]
    L1[k] = cand1 if cand1 >= cand2 else cand2
Need note word1[p] != word2[k] uses char comparison; word2[k] char. Good.

Let's test cand2 with T_mismatch maybe n. If T_mismatch = n, p=n-1. If word1[n-1] != c, cand2=n-1. If word1[n-1]==c, prev_diff[n-1] largest non-c before. Good. If T_mismatch = -1, no p. If T_mismatch=0, no index <0. Good.

Now, is exact_start[k] maybe > L1[k]? No L1 includes exact, so L1[k] >= exact[k]? Let's check recurrence: cand1 uses L1[k+1] and char equal. Because threshold L1[k+1] >= exact[k+1], largest char equal < L1[k+1] is at least largest char equal < exact[k+1]. So cand1 >= exact[k]. Thus L1[k] >= exact[k]. Good.

Now, greedy scanning. Need maybe use precomputed arrays and char comparisons. We can implement:

ans = []
prev = -1
rem = 1
for k in range(m):
    target = word2[k]
    c = ord(target)-97
    found = -1
    # scan i from prev+1 to n-1
    # Need maybe limit? Could scan all n; if no found return [].
    for i in range(prev+1, n):
        if rem == 1:
            if word1[i] == target:
                if i < L1[k+1]:
                    found = i
                    break
            else:
                if i < exact[k+1]:
                    found = i
                    break
        else: # rem ==0
            if word1[i] == target and i < exact[k+1]:
                found = i
                break
    if found == -1:
        return []
    ans.append(found)
    if word1[found] != target:
        rem = 0
    prev = found

Will this scan total O(n)? At each k, loop starts at prev+1 and breaks at found. Since prev set to found, next loop starts after found. So each index considered at most once? Wait if for some k, it scans multiple i and finds found; those scanned i are not appended. Next k starts at found+1, so scanned failed indices before found are not scanned again. Yes total scanned indices <= n. But what if for a k, it scans to n and returns [] after scanning many; total <=n. Good.

Need ensure if rem=1 and word1[i]==target and i < L1[k+1], we choose it. But what if i < L1[k+1] false, but i < exact[k+1] true and word1[i]==target? If i < exact[k+1] true but L1[k+1] false, can that happen? L1[k+1] >= exact[k+1], so if i < exact then i < L1. So no. Good.

Implementation plan:

from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def validSequence(self, word1: str, word2: str) -> List[int]:
        n, m = len(word1), len(word2)
        pos = [[] for _ in range(26)]
        prev_diff = [-1] * n
        last = [-1] * 26
        max1_pos = -1
        max1_char = -1
        max2_pos = -1
        for i, ch in enumerate(word1):
            c = ord(ch) - 97
            pos[c].append(i)
            if max1_char == c:
                prev_diff[i] = max2_pos
            else:
                prev_diff[i] = max1_pos
            # update last
            last[c] = i
            # update top two positions with different chars
            if i > max1_pos:
                if c != max1_char:
                    max2_pos = max1_pos
                    max1_pos = i
                    max1_char = c
                else:
                    max1_pos = i
            elif i > max2_pos and c != max1_char:
                max2_pos = i
        # Wait: Is prev_diff computed correctly before updating top two? For current i, prev_diff should be largest index < i with char != current. We use max1/max2 before updating with current. Yes. Then update. Good.
        # But if max1_char == c, prev_diff=max2_pos. If max1_char != c, prev_diff=max1_pos. This works because max1 is largest position among chars != c if max1_char != c; if max1_char == c, max2 is largest position among chars != c. Need ensure max2_pos is always largest position with char != max1_char. Our update maintains. Let's verify with scenario where c == max1_char and i > max1_pos, max1_pos updated to i, max2 remains largest different from old max1 (same char), so okay. If c != max1_char and i > max1_pos, old max1 becomes max2, new max1 c; max2 char old max1 != c, okay. If c != max1_char and i <= max1_pos but i > max2_pos, update max2 to i; max2 char c != max1_char, okay. If c == max1_char and i <= max1_pos, no update. Good.
        # However, if max1_char == -1 initially, c != -1, prev_diff=max1_pos=-1. update: i > -1 and c != -1 -> max2=-1, max1=0,max1_char=c. Good.

        exact = [0]*(m+1)
        exact[m] = n
        for k in range(m-1, -1, -1):
            c = ord(word2[k]) - 97
            T = exact[k+1]
            lst = pos[c]
            idx = bisect_left(lst, T) - 1
            exact[k] = lst[idx] if idx >= 0 else -1

        L1 = [0]*(m+1)
        L1[m] = n
        for k in range(m-1, -1, -1):
            c = ord(word2[k]) - 97
            T_exact = L1[k+1]
            T_mismatch = exact[k+1]
            lst = pos[c]
            idx = bisect_left(lst, T_exact) - 1
            cand1 = lst[idx] if idx >= 0 else -1
            cand2 = -1
            if T_mismatch > 0:
                p = T_mismatch - 1
                if word1[p] != word2[k]:
                    cand2 = p
                else:
                    cand2 = prev_diff[p]
            L1[k] = cand1 if cand1 >= cand2 else cand2

        ans = []
        prev = -1
        rem = 1
        for k in range(m):
            target = word2[k]
            # maybe local variables for speed
            if rem == 1:
                limit_equal = L1[k+1]
                limit_mismatch = exact[k+1]
                found = -1
                for i in range(prev+1, n):
                    if word1[i] == target:
                        if i < limit_equal:
                            found = i
                            break
                    else:
                        if i < limit_mismatch:
                            found = i
                            break
            else:
                limit = exact[k+1]
                found = -1
                for i in range(prev+1, n):
                    if word1[i] == target and i < limit:
                        found = i
                        break
            if found == -1:
                return []
            ans.append(found)
            if word1[found] != target:
                rem = 0
            prev = found
        return ans

Need check prev_diff top-two update with last array? We don't actually use last except maybe not needed. We can remove last to save memory. In top-two update, we don't need last. But for prev_diff, we need max1/max2 before update. We don't need last. So remove last. But wait, if max1_char == c, prev_diff=max2_pos. Suppose there are multiple different chars; max2_pos is largest position with char != max1_char. But is max2_pos always the largest position with char != max1_char, even if max2_pos char maybe same as max1_char? We maintain invariant. Let's prove update:
Invariant before processing i: max1_pos is largest index < i, max1_char is its char. max2_pos is largest index < i with char != max1_char (or -1). Then prev_diff:
- If current char c == max1_char, largest index < i with char != c is max2_pos (since max1 has same char c, so not different; max2 is largest different from max1_char=c). Good.
- If c != max1_char, largest index < i with char != c is max1_pos (since max1 char != c; any larger different? max1 is largest overall, so yes). Good.
Then update:
- If i > max1_pos:
   If c != max1_char: new max1 is i char c. Old max1 is largest index < i with char != c? Actually old max1 char != c, so it becomes max2. max2_pos = max1_pos. Good.
   If c == max1_char: new max1 i same char; max2 remains largest index < i with char != c (same as old max2). Good.
- Else (i <= max1_pos):
   If c != max1_char and i > max2_pos: new max2 i. If i <= max2_pos, no update. If c == max1_char, i is same as max1_char and not larger than max1, so not max2. Good.
This works. We don't need last.

But wait, in update when i > max1_pos and c != max1_char, what if max1_pos = -1 and max1_char=-1 initially; old max1=-1, max2=-1. Good. If max1_char=-1 and c? c != -1, max2=-1. Good.
What if max1_pos = -1 but max1_char = -1, and c maybe -1? c never -1. Good.

Now, is prev_diff[p] for p where word1[p] == target and there is a non-target before p but that non-target might be at index p? No, prev_diff is < p. Good.

Let's test top-two prev_diff with a sequence:
word1 = "abac"
i0 a: max1=-1, max1_char=-1, prev=-1. update max1=0 char a, max2=-1.
i1 b: max1_char a != b, prev=max1_pos=0. update i1>0 and b!=a -> max2=0, max1=1 char b.
i2 a: max1_char b != a, prev=max1_pos=1. update i2>1 and a!=b -> max2=1, max1=2 char a.
i3 c: max1_char a != c, prev=max1_pos=2. update i3>2 and c!=a -> max2=2, max1=3 char c.
prev_diff: [ -1, 0, 1, 2 ]. For i2 a, largest index <2 with char != a is1 (b), correct. For i3 c, largest <3 with !=c is2 (a), correct.
word1="aaaa":
i0 prev -1; max1=0 a.
i1 max1_char a==a, prev=max2=-1; update max1=1.
i2 prev=-1; max1=2.
i3 prev=-1. Correct no different before.
word1="aaab":

i1 -1; max1=1 a
i2 -1; max1=2 a

i3 b: prev=max2=0; max1=3. prev_diff for b at2/3 is0 (a), correct.

But wait: What if at some step k, we scan from prev+1 to found, found is appended. Next step starts at found+1. So each index is considered exactly once as a candidate for selection? No, because later k requires larger indices; those infeasible indices are < found < later start, so cannot be selected later. So no need to reconsider. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if `word1` length n and `word2` length m
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
is empty.</think>```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def validSequence(self, word1: str, word2: str) -> List[int]:
        n, m = len(word1), len(word2)

        pos = [[] for _ in range(26)]
        prev_diff = [-1] * n

        max1_pos = -1
        max1_char = -1
        max2_pos = -1

        for i, ch in enumerate(word1):
            c = ord(ch) - 97
            pos[c].append(i)

            if max1_char == c:
                prev_diff[i] = max2_pos
            else:
                prev_diff[i] = max1_pos

            if i > max1_pos:
                if c != max1_char:
                    max2_pos = max1_pos
                    max1_pos = i
                    max1_char = c
                else:
                    max1_pos = i
            elif i > max2_pos and c != max1_char:
                max2_pos = i

        exact_start = [0] * (m + 1)
        exact_start[m] = n

        for k in range(m - 1, -1, -1):
            c = ord(word2[k]) - 97
            lst = pos[c]
            idx = bisect_left(lst, exact_start[k + 1]) - 1
            exact_start[k] = lst[idx] if idx >= 0 else -1

        one_mismatch_start = [0] * (m + 1)
        one_mismatch_start[m] = n

        for k in range(m - 1, -1, -1):
            c = ord(word2[k]) - 97
            lst = pos[c]

            idx = bisect_left(lst, one_mismatch_start[k + 1]) - 1
            cand_exact = lst[idx] if idx >= 0 else -1

            cand_mismatch = -1
            limit = exact_start[k + 1]
            if limit > 0:
                p = limit - 1
                if word1[p] != word2[k]:
                    cand_mismatch = p
                else:
                    cand_mismatch = prev_diff[p]

            one_mismatch_start[k] = max(cand_exact, cand_mismatch)

        ans = []
        prev = -1
        rem = 1

        for k in range(m):
            target = word2[k]
            found = -1

            if rem == 1:
                limit_equal = one_mismatch_start[k + 1]
                limit_mismatch = exact_start[k + 1]

                for i in range(prev + 1, n):
                    if word1[i] == target:
                        if i < limit_equal:
                            found = i
                            break
                    else:
                        if i < limit_mismatch:
                            found = i
                            break
            else:
                limit = exact_start[k + 1]
                for i in range(prev + 1, n):
                    if word1[i] == target and i < limit:
                        found = i
                        break

            if found == -1:
                return []

            ans.append(found)
            if word1[found] != target:
                rem = 0
            prev = found

        return ans
```
